Yeah… I guess I have. I mean, I don’t always think of it that way because I tell myself I’m just busy or tired, but I’ve missed a lot of stuff I used to show up for.

Like basketball, and those dance nights—I used to go even if I wasn’t amazing at it, it was just something to do. And at church, I was helping with the STEM mentoring thing, which I actually did care about, but I’ve mostly stopped showing up. It’s embarrassing because those kids notice when people aren’t there, you know? And then I start thinking about how my schedule’s weird, and work already drains me, and then suddenly it feels like too much to even get there.

Work has been affected too. I’ve gotten written warnings because I’ve missed shifts, and I’ve turned in inspection paperwork late. The documentation part is so repetitive, but it still has to be done, and I just… I don’t know. I keep letting things slip.

Yeah… yeah, I think so. It’s not even always big things. Like filling out the inspection logs at work, which shouldn’t be that hard, just feels like this huge thing sitting there. And then I put it off, and it gets worse.

I haven’t really noticed one specific thing where I’m like, “I don’t enjoy this anymore.”\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when something used to bring you joy or satisfaction—like a specific activity, moment, or interaction—and whether that still feels rewarding to you now?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… I don’t know. I feel like I should have an answer, like, “Oh, I used to love this and now I don’t,” but nothing really comes to mind clearly.\n\nI mean, I used to like little things, like just talking with people after church or having a decent day where work didn’t feel so long. But even saying that, I’m not sure if it was joy exactly or just… routine. Sometimes I think maybe I’m overthinking the word “rewarding.” Like, what does it count as if something is still nice but it doesn’t really stick with me?\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going in circles. I don’t think I can point to one specific thing and say for sure that it used to make me happy and now it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you ever stopped doing something you used to do regularly, even if you didn’t necessarily love it before, just because it felt like too much effort or didn’t seem worth it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. I mean, I don’t always think of it that way because I tell myself I’m just busy or tired, but I’ve missed a lot of stuff I used to show up for.\n\nLike basketball, and those dance nights—I used to go even if I wasn’t amazing at it, it was just something to do. And at church, I was helping with the STEM mentoring thing, which I actually did care about, but I’ve mostly stopped showing up. It’s embarrassing because those kids notice when people aren’t there, you know? And then I start thinking about how my schedule’s weird, and work already drains me, and then suddenly it feels like too much to even get there.\n\nWork has been affected too. I’ve gotten written warnings because I’ve missed shifts, and I’ve turned in inspection paperwork late. The documentation part is so repetitive, but it still has to be done, and I just… I don’t know. I keep letting things slip.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you noticed that you’ve had significantly less energy than usual, or that even small tasks feel exhausting to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. It’s not even always big things. Like filling out the inspection logs at work, which shouldn’t be that hard, just feels like this huge thing sitting there. And then I put it off, and it gets worse.\n\nEven driving to basketball, which isn’t like climbing a mountain or anything, it just feels like more energy than I have. Sometimes even washing my hair feels like… I don’t know, like I have to plan around it.\n\nAnd I’ll think, okay, I just need a day off, I need to rest. But then on my days off I rest, and it doesn’t really feel like I’m refilled. It’s like my battery never gets back up all the way.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you had days where you felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, not just occasionally or in response to specific events?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of why I’m here, I think. It’s not like one bad week or something that happened and then I got over it. For the last couple years I just feel low most of the time.\n\nEven when I’m at work, or at church, or around people I care about, it’s like there’s this heavy, empty feeling sitting there. Sometimes I can still talk and act normal, and people probably wouldn’t know, but it doesn’t really lift. It’s just kind of… there, most days.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… definitely. I mean, I keep trying to tell myself it’s not that bad, but it kind of is.

At work, I’ve gotten written warnings because I’ve missed shifts, and I’ve turned in inspection documentation late. Which is stupid because it’s not like I don’t know how to do it—I do. It’s just… I don’t get it done. And then once you get one warning, you start feeling weird walking in, like everyone knows you’re messing up, even if they’re just doing their own job.

And socially, I’ve mostly stopped showing up for stuff. Basketball, dance nights… and the church STEM mentoring, which I actually cared about. I used to like being there with the kids, helping them figure things out, and now I’ll think about going and then just not. Or I’ll tell myself I’ll go next time, and then next time becomes the same thing.

So yeah, it’s affected things. More than I like admitting.

Yeah… I mean, I think so. It’s hard to separate it into neat chunks, like “this month was bad and that month wasn’t,” because it’s kind of been the background for the last couple years.

Sometimes I’m talking and smiling, and I remember thinking once during coffee after church—someone was talking about their cousin’s wedding or something, and I was nodding along—but inside I just felt… low. So yeah, not just a day here or there. It’s been most days, for a long time.

I haven’t really noticed one specific thing where I’m like, “I don’t enjoy this anymore.”\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when something used to bring you joy or satisfaction—like a specific activity, moment, or interaction—and whether that still feels rewarding to you now?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… I don’t know. I feel like I should have an answer, like, “Oh, I used to love this and now I don’t,” but nothing really comes to mind clearly.\n\nI mean, I used to like little things, like just talking with people after church or having a decent day where work didn’t feel so long. But even saying that, I’m not sure if it was joy exactly or just… routine. Sometimes I think maybe I’m overthinking the word “rewarding.” Like, what does it count as if something is still nice but it doesn’t really stick with me?\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going in circles. I don’t think I can point to one specific thing and say for sure that it used to make me happy and now it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you ever stopped doing something you used to do regularly, even if you didn’t necessarily love it before, just because it felt like too much effort or didn’t seem worth it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. I mean, I don’t always think of it that way because I tell myself I’m just busy or tired, but I’ve missed a lot of stuff I used to show up for.\n\nLike basketball, and those dance nights—I used to go even if I wasn’t amazing at it, it was just something to do. And at church, I was helping with the STEM mentoring thing, which I actually did care about, but I’ve mostly stopped showing up. It’s embarrassing because those kids notice when people aren’t there, you know? And then I start thinking about how my schedule’s weird, and work already drains me, and then suddenly it feels like too much to even get there.\n\nWork has been affected too. I’ve gotten written warnings because I’ve missed shifts, and I’ve turned in inspection paperwork late. The documentation part is so repetitive, but it still has to be done, and I just… I don’t know. I keep letting things slip.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you noticed that you’ve had significantly less energy than usual, or that even small tasks feel exhausting to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. It’s not even always big things. Like filling out the inspection logs at work, which shouldn’t be that hard, just feels like this huge thing sitting there. And then I put it off, and it gets worse.\n\nEven driving to basketball, which isn’t like climbing a mountain or anything, it just feels like more energy than I have. Sometimes even washing my hair feels like… I don’t know, like I have to plan around it.\n\nAnd I’ll think, okay, I just need a day off, I need to rest. But then on my days off I rest, and it doesn’t really feel like I’m refilled. It’s like my battery never gets back up all the way.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you had days where you felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, not just occasionally or in response to specific events?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of why I’m here, I think. It’s not like one bad week or something that happened and then I got over it. For the last couple years I just feel low most of the time.\n\nEven when I’m at work, or at church, or around people I care about, it’s like there’s this heavy, empty feeling sitting there. Sometimes I can still talk and act normal, and people probably wouldn’t know, but it doesn’t really lift. It’s just kind of… there, most days.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you noticed a significant decline in your ability to function at work or in your social life because of how you’ve been feeling?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I mean, I keep trying to tell myself it’s not that bad, but it kind of is.\n\nAt work, I’ve gotten written warnings because I’ve missed shifts, and I’ve turned in inspection documentation late. Which is stupid because it’s not like I don’t know how to do it—I do. It’s just… I don’t get it done. And then once you get one warning, you start feeling weird walking in, like everyone knows you’re messing up, even if they’re just doing their own job.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve mostly stopped showing up for stuff. Basketball, dance nights… and the church STEM mentoring, which I actually cared about. I used to like being there with the kids, helping them figure things out, and now I’ll think about going and then just not. Or I’ll tell myself I’ll go next time, and then next time becomes the same thing.\n\nSo yeah, it’s affected things. More than I like admitting.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there have been times when you felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for weeks or months at a time over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. It’s hard to separate it into neat chunks, like “this month was bad and that month wasn’t,” because it’s kind of been the background for the last couple years.\n\nLike, I’ll be at work doing my normal routine, or at church, or even with people I really care about, and there’s still this heavy, empty feeling there. Sometimes I’m talking and smiling, and I remember thinking once during coffee after church—someone was talking about their cousin’s wedding or something, and I was nodding along—but inside I just felt… low. Like it didn’t match what was happening around me.\n\nSo yeah, not just a day here or there. It’s been most days, for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you often felt worthless, guilty, or overly responsible for things, even when others wouldn’t see it that way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I feel bad about stuff, but I’m not sure if I’d call it “worthless” exactly.\n\nLike, I’ll mess something up and then I’ll replay it a lot, but then I also think everybody does that, right? My cousin once got in this big argument over who was supposed to bring chairs to a cookout, and she still talks about it like three years later, which is kind of funny but also—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I’m not sure how to answer. I do feel guilty sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than normal or if I’m just thinking too much about it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight—like eating much less or much more than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s been kind of all over the place.\n\nMost days I either don’t eat until my shift is almost done because nothing sounds good, and then by the time I realize it I’m shaky or just tired. Or I’ll end up getting, like, vending machine stuff late—not because I really want it, more because I’m drained and it’s there.\n\nAnd my weight hasn’t been steady. I don’t track it closely, but my work pants fit different from month to month, which is annoying because those uniforms already fit weird anyway. But yeah, it’s noticeable.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Which is stupid because it’s not like I don’t know how to do it—I do. It’s just… I don’t get it done. And then once you get one warning, you start feeling weird walking in, like everyone knows you’re messing up, even if they’re just doing their own job.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve mostly stopped showing up for stuff. Even when I’m at work, or at church, or around people I really care about, it’s like there’s this heavy, empty feeling that doesn’t really go away.\n\nI mean, work is kind of repetitive anyway—I inspect and sort things, so maybe some people would feel drained doing that—but this feels bigger than that. It just kind of follows me everywhere.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any loss of interest or pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy, like hobbies, work, or spending time with loved ones?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean… I still do things, like I’ll go to work and sometimes see people or go to church, but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve “lost interest” exactly. It’s more like everything just feels kind of far away, if that makes sense.\n\nLike even with hobbies—well, I used to try different stuff when I was in school, and then I got busy, and my schedule got weird, and honestly sometimes I can’t tell if I stopped because I don’t enjoy it or just because life got repetitive. Work is like that too, just the same tasks over and over, and then I start thinking maybe everyone feels that way. So… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed one specific thing where I’m like, “I don’t enjoy this anymore.”\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when something used to bring you joy or satisfaction—like a specific activity, moment, or interaction—and whether that still feels rewarding to you now?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… I don’t know. I feel like I should have an answer, like, “Oh, I used to love this and now I don’t,” but nothing really comes to mind clearly.\n\nI mean, I used to like little things, like just talking with people after church or having a decent day where work didn’t feel so long. But even saying that, I’m not sure if it was joy exactly or just… routine. Sometimes I think maybe I’m overthinking the word “rewarding.” Like, what does it count as if something is still nice but it doesn’t really stick with me?\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going in circles. I don’t think I can point to one specific thing and say for sure that it used to make me happy and now it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you ever stopped doing something you used to do regularly, even if you didn’t necessarily love it before, just because it felt like too much effort or didn’t seem worth it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. I mean, I don’t always think of it that way because I tell myself I’m just busy or tired, but I’ve missed a lot of stuff I used to show up for.\n\nLike basketball, and those dance nights—I used to go even if I wasn’t amazing at it, it was just something to do. And at church, I was helping with the STEM mentoring thing, which I actually did care about, but I’ve mostly stopped showing up. It’s embarrassing because those kids notice when people aren’t there, you know? And then I start thinking about how my schedule’s weird, and work already drains me, and then suddenly it feels like too much to even get there.\n\nWork has been affected too. I’ve gotten written warnings because I’ve missed shifts, and I’ve turned in inspection paperwork late. The documentation part is so repetitive, but it still has to be done, and I just… I don’t know. I keep letting things slip.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you noticed that you’ve had significantly less energy than usual, or that even small tasks feel exhausting to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. It’s not even always big things. Like filling out the inspection logs at work, which shouldn’t be that hard, just feels like this huge thing sitting there. And then I put it off, and it gets worse.\n\nEven driving to basketball, which isn’t like climbing a mountain or anything, it just feels like more energy than I have. Sometimes even washing my hair feels like… I don’t know, like I have to plan around it.\n\nAnd I’ll think, okay, I just need a day off, I need to rest. But then on my days off I rest, and it doesn’t really feel like I’m refilled. It’s like my battery never gets back up all the way.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you had days where you felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, not just occasionally or in response to specific events?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of why I’m here, I think. It’s not like one bad week or something that happened and then I got over it. For the last couple years I just feel low most of the time.\n\nEven when I’m at work, or at church, or around people I care about, it’s like there’s this heavy, empty feeling sitting there. Sometimes I can still talk and act normal, and people probably wouldn’t know, but it doesn’t really lift. It’s just kind of… there, most days.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you noticed a significant decline in your ability to function at work or in your social life because of how you’ve been feeling?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I mean, I keep trying to tell myself it’s not that bad, but it kind of is.\n\nAt work, I’ve gotten written warnings because I’ve missed shifts, and I’ve turned in inspection documentation late. Which is stupid because it’s not like I don’t know how to do it—I do. It’s just… I don’t get it done. And then once you get one warning, you start feeling weird walking in, like everyone knows you’re messing up, even if they’re just doing their own job.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve mostly stopped showing up for stuff. Basketball, dance nights… and the church STEM mentoring, which I actually cared about. I used to like being there with the kids, helping them figure things out, and now I’ll think about going and then just not. Or I’ll tell myself I’ll go next time, and then next time becomes the same thing.\n\nSo yeah, it’s affected things. More than I like admitting.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there have been times when you felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for weeks or months at a time over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. It’s hard to separate it into neat chunks, like “this month was bad and that month wasn’t,” because it’s kind of been the background for the last couple years.\n\nLike, I’ll be at work doing my normal routine, or at church, or even with people I really care about, and there’s still this heavy, empty feeling there. Sometimes I’m talking and smiling, and I remember thinking once during coffee after church—someone was talking about their cousin’s wedding or something, and I was nodding along—but inside I just felt… low. Like it didn’t match what was happening around me.\n\nSo yeah, not just a day here or there. It’s been most days, for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you often felt worthless, guilty, or overly responsible for things, even when others wouldn’t see it that way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I feel bad about stuff, but I’m not sure if I’d call it “worthless” exactly.\n\nLike, I’ll mess something up and then I’ll replay it a lot, but then I also think everybody does that, right? My cousin once got in this big argument over who was supposed to bring chairs to a cookout, and she still talks about it like three years later, which is kind of funny but also—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I’m not sure how to answer. I do feel guilty sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than normal or if I’m just thinking too much about it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight—like eating much less or much more than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s been kind of all over the place.\n\nMost days I either don’t eat until my shift is almost done because nothing sounds good, and then by the time I realize it I’m shaky or just tired. Or I’ll end up getting, like, vending machine stuff late—not because I really want it, more because I’m drained and it’s there.\n\nAnd my weight hasn’t been steady. I don’t track it closely, but my work pants fit different from month to month, which is annoying because those uniforms already fit weird anyway. But yeah, it’s noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you noticed that your movements or speech have slowed down, or that you’ve felt restless or unable to sit still for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t paid attention to that, I guess.\n\nI mean, sometimes at work people are always moving fast, checking parts and sorting things, and it can feel like everyone’s in a rush anyway, so I wouldn’t know if I’m slower or if the day is just dragging. And sitting still… I don’t know, I’ll shift around, but I’ve kind of always done that, like in school I used to tap my foot and my teachers hated it.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed anything clear like that.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if there have been periods of at least two consecutive years where you felt down or low most days, and during that time, you also had other symptoms like low energy, changes in appetite, or difficulty functioning?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s been, like, about two years and four months now, if I’m counting right.\n\nIt kind of started after I had to stop taking my community college classes, because I couldn’t make the money and my work schedule line up. I kept thinking I’d go back the next semester, and then the next one, and then it just… didn’t happen. And there was this whole thing with financial aid forms too—sorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nBut yeah, since around then, it’s been most days. Not really just a bad week or something.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if there was a time in the past two years when you felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks straight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s more than two weeks, honestly. I’m here because for the last couple years I just feel low almost all the time.\n\nEven when I’m at work, or at church, or around people I care about, it’s like there’s this heavy, empty feeling that doesn’t really lift. Sometimes I’ll be standing there talking to someone and I’m still kind of… sunk inside, if that makes sense. So yes, there have definitely been stretches where it was most of the day, nearly every day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you noticed that your movements or speech have slowed down, or that you’ve felt restless or unable to sit still for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t paid attention to that, I guess.\n\nI mean, sometimes at work people are always moving fast, checking parts and sorting things, and it can feel like everyone’s in a rush anyway, so I wouldn’t know if I’m slower or if the day is just dragging. And sitting still… I don’t know, I’ll shift around, but I’ve kind of always done that, like in school I used to tap my foot and my teachers hated it.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed anything clear like that.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if there have been periods of at least two consecutive years where you felt down or low most days, and during that time, you also had other symptoms like low energy, changes in appetite, or difficulty functioning?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s been, like, about two years and four months now, if I’m counting right.\n\nIt kind of started after I had to stop taking my community college classes, because I couldn’t make the money and my work schedule line up. I kept thinking I’d go back the next semester, and then the next one, and then it just… didn’t happen. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really… I mean, I’m trying to think of a stretch like that, but this has kind of been going on the whole time, about two years and four months now.

It started after I had to stop taking my community college classes because I couldn’t make the money and my work schedule line up. I kept telling myself I’d figure it out, and then time just kept passing. So… no, I don’t think I had more than two months where things felt noticeably better.

It’s like my battery never gets back up all the way.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you had days where you felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, not just occasionally or in response to specific events?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of why I’m here, I think. It’s just kind of… there, most days.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you noticed a significant decline in your ability to function at work or in your social life because of how you’ve been feeling?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. Which is stupid because it’s not like I don’t know how to do it—I do. It’s just… I don’t get it done. And then once you get one warning, you start feeling weird walking in, like everyone knows you’re messing up, even if they’re just doing their own job.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve mostly stopped showing up for stuff. It’s like my battery never gets back up all the way.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you had days where you felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, not just occasionally or in response to specific events?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of why I’m here, I think. Sometimes I’m talking and smiling, and I remember thinking once during coffee after church—someone was talking about their cousin’s wedding or something, and I was nodding along—but inside I just felt… low. It’s embarrassing because those kids notice when people aren’t there, you know? And then I start thinking about how my schedule’s weird, and work already drains me, and then suddenly it feels like too much to even get there.\n\nWork has been affected too. I’ve gotten written warnings because I’ve missed shifts, and I’ve turned in inspection paperwork late. The documentation part is so repetitive, but it still has to be done, and I just… I don’t know. I keep letting things slip.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you noticed that you’ve had significantly less energy than usual, or that even small tasks feel exhausting to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. It’s not even always big things. Like filling out the inspection logs at work, which shouldn’t be that hard, just feels like this huge thing sitting there. And then I put it off, and it gets worse.\n\nEven driving to basketball, which isn’t like climbing a mountain or anything, it just feels like more energy than I have. Sometimes even washing my hair feels like… I don’t know, like I have to plan around it.\n\nAnd I’ll think, okay, I just need a day off, I need to rest. But then on my days off I rest, and it doesn’t really feel like I’m refilled. It’s like my battery never gets back up all the way.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you had days where you felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, not just occasionally or in response to specific events?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of why I’m here, I think. It’s not like one bad week or something that happened and then I got over it. For the last couple years I just feel low most of the time.\n\nEven when I’m at work, or at church, or around people I care about, it’s like there’s this heavy, empty feeling sitting there. Sometimes I can still talk and act normal, and people probably wouldn’t know, but it doesn’t really lift. It’s just kind of… there, most days.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you noticed a significant decline in your ability to function at work or in your social life because of how you’ve been feeling?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I mean, I keep trying to tell myself it’s not that bad, but it kind of is.\n\nAt work, I’ve gotten written warnings because I’ve missed shifts, and I’ve turned in inspection documentation late. Which is stupid because it’s not like I don’t know how to do it—I do. It’s just… I don’t get it done. And then once you get one warning, you start feeling weird walking in, like everyone knows you’re messing up, even if they’re just doing their own job.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve mostly stopped showing up for stuff. Basketball, dance nights… and the church STEM mentoring, which I actually cared about. I used to like being there with the kids, helping them figure things out, and now I’ll think about going and then just not. Or I’ll tell myself I’ll go next time, and then next time becomes the same thing.\n\nSo yeah, it’s affected things. More than I like admitting.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there have been times when you felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for weeks or months at a time over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. It’s hard to separate it into neat chunks, like “this month was bad and that month wasn’t,” because it’s kind of been the background for the last couple years.\n\nLike, I’ll be at work doing my normal routine, or at church, or even with people I really care about, and there’s still this heavy, empty feeling there. Sometimes I’m talking and smiling, and I remember thinking once during coffee after church—someone was talking about their cousin’s wedding or something, and I was nodding along—but inside I just felt… low. Like it didn’t match what was happening around me.\n\nSo yeah, not just a day here or there. It’s been most days, for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you often felt worthless, guilty, or overly responsible for things, even when others wouldn’t see it that way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I feel bad about stuff, but I’m not sure if I’d call it “worthless” exactly.\n\nLike, I’ll mess something up and then I’ll replay it a lot, but then I also think everybody does that, right? My cousin once got in this big argument over who was supposed to bring chairs to a cookout, and she still talks about it like three years later, which is kind of funny but also—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I’m not sure how to answer. I do feel guilty sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than normal or if I’m just thinking too much about it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight—like eating much less or much more than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s been kind of all over the place.\n\nMost days I either don’t eat until my shift is almost done because nothing sounds good, and then by the time I realize it I’m shaky or just tired. Or I’ll end up getting, like, vending machine stuff late—not because I really want it, more because I’m drained and it’s there.\n\nAnd my weight hasn’t been steady. I don’t track it closely, but my work pants fit different from month to month, which is annoying because those uniforms already fit weird anyway. But yeah, it’s noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you noticed that your movements or speech have slowed down, or that you’ve felt restless or unable to sit still for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t paid attention to that, I guess.\n\nI mean, sometimes at work people are always moving fast, checking parts and sorting things, and it can feel like everyone’s in a rush anyway, so I wouldn’t know if I’m slower or if the day is just dragging. And sitting still… I don’t know, I’ll shift around, but I’ve kind of always done that, like in school I used to tap my foot and my teachers hated it.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed anything clear like that.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if there have been periods of at least two consecutive years where you felt down or low most days, and during that time, you also had other symptoms like low energy, changes in appetite, or difficulty functioning?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s been, like, about two years and four months now, if I’m counting right.\n\nIt kind of started after I had to stop taking my community college classes, because I couldn’t make the money and my work schedule line up. I kept thinking I’d go back the next semester, and then the next one, and then it just… didn’t happen. And there was this whole thing with financial aid forms too—sorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nBut yeah, since around then, it’s been most days. Not really just a bad week or something.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if there was a time in the past two years when you felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks straight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s more than two weeks, honestly. I’m here because for the last couple years I just feel low almost all the time.\n\nEven when I’m at work, or at church, or around people I care about, it’s like there’s this heavy, empty feeling that doesn’t really lift. Sometimes I’ll be standing there talking to someone and I’m still kind of… sunk inside, if that makes sense. So yes, there have definitely been stretches where it was most of the day, nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have there been any periods where you felt like your mood was noticeably better for more than two months at a time?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I’m trying to think of a stretch like that, but this has kind of been going on the whole time, about two years and four months now.\n\nIt started after I had to stop taking my community college classes because I couldn’t make the money and my work schedule line up. I kept telling myself I’d figure it out, and then time just kept passing. So… no, I don’t think I had more than two months where things felt noticeably better.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you had any period that lasted at least two months where you felt close to your usual self, with no significant symptoms of depression?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. It’s been going on for about two years and four months, since I had to stop taking community college classes. It was because of money and my work schedule not lining up, and I kept thinking I’d fix it later, but… yeah, I don’t think there was a two-month stretch where I felt back to normal."
